\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
\end{array}

Figure 3:
The sphere
S
corresponds to, the sphere at the end of the straight line in the
D_{n}
diagram, and the third sphere in the
E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
diagrams, respectively.

By Theorem
B
, these configurations do not occur. Thus, DE singularities cannot occur on
X
. Next, assume
n
is even and
\frac{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}X+1}{2r_{X}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
where
r_{X}
is the Fano index. Similarly to the even
n
case, by Theorem
B
, an
A_{3}
-configuration is prohibited, so
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
singularities are the only two simple singularities that can occur on
X
.

Case of positive modality.

The argument goes similarly to the case of modality zero, i.e. simple singularities. We argue by contradiction; assume that
X
degenerates to a Fano variety
X_{0}
with a positive modality singularity. Then
(X,\omega)
contains a configuration of Lagrangian spheres coming from a positive modality singularity. From Proposition
2.5.4
, we know that the vanishing cycle of an isolated hypersurface singularity with positive modality includes the vanishing cycle of one of the three parabolic singularities
\widetilde{E}_{6},\widetilde{E}_{7},\widetilde{E}_{8}
. Keating studied the geometry of the vanishing cycles of the three parabolic singularities
\widetilde{E}_{6},\widetilde{E}_{7},\widetilde{E}_{8}
and proved that the Lagrangian spheres that appear in the vanishing cycles of the three parabolic singularities intersect as in the Dynkin diagram of Gabrielov
[
Kea15
, Figure 4]
. In the Dynkin diagram of Gabrielov
[
Kea15
, Figure 4]
, one can find a collection of four Lagrangian spheres
\{S,S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}\}
just as in the case of simple singularities: there is a Lagrangian sphere
S
that intersects three other Lagrangian spheres
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
,

|S\cap S_{j}|=1,\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant 3.

Remark 3.3.5.
The Lagrangian spheres
S,S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
correspond to the Lagrangian spheres 4,1,2,3, respectively, in Keating’s numbering in
[
Kea15
, Figure 4]
.

The rest of the proof is exactly the same as the modality zero case. We complete the proof of Theorem
A
by proving Claim
3.3.3
. As we assume that
X_{0}
is Fano, which is equivalent to the ampleness of the line bundle
\Omega_{X_{0}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{C}}|_{X_{0}}
, Claim
3.3.3
is a direct consequence of
[
Gro61
, Théorème 4.7.1]
(see also
[
Laz04
, Theorem 1.2.17]
). Note that
[
Gro61
, Théorème 4.7.1]
requires the morphism
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
to be proper and
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
to be a line bundle. The properness of
\pi
is satisfied, as all the fibers of
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
are compact. As for verifying that the dualizing sheaf
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
is indeed a line bundle, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{X}
is Gorenstein (see Remark
2.4
), and this follows from the following proposition.

Proposition 3.3.6
.

Let
X,Y
be varieties and
\pi:X\to Y
a flat morphism. If
Y
is Gorenstein and all the fibers
\pi^{-1}(y),\ y\in Y
are Gorenstein, then
X
is also Gorenstein.

In our case

•

X_{t},\ t\neq 0
is a smooth variety, and smooth varieties are Gorenstein,

•

X_{0}
has at most isolated hypersurface singularities, so it is Gorenstein,

and thus all the fibers of the flat morphism of the degeneration
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
are Gorenstein. Thus,
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
is a line bundle from Proposition
3.3.6
. We have completed the proof of Theorem
A
.

∎

Remark 3.3.7.

It would be very interesting to study the spectral rigidity of singularities that are not isolated hypersurface singularities, for example cyclic quotient singularities. They are ‘similar’ to the
A_{m}
-singularities in the sense that their vanishing cycles are
A_{m}
-configurations attached to a certain singular Lagrangian called the Lagrangian pinwheel, c.f.
[
Eva
, Section 1.2.3]
. In this case, it is interesting to see if a property similar to Lemma
3.2.1
would hold between a Lagrangian pinwheel and a Lagrangian sphere. Studying the spectral rigidity of Lagrangian pinwheels is also an interesting topic. If one could prove results similar to Lemma
3.1.4
for Lagrangian pinwheels, it will bring new applications to Hofer geometry.

3.4
Proof of Theorem
C

In this section, we prove Theorem
C
.

Proof of Theorem
C
.

Denote the Lagrangian spheres forming the
A_{m}
-configuration by

\mathscr{S}_{A_{m}}:=\{S_{1},\cdots,S_{m}\}.

As
QH(X,\omega)
is semi-simple, there is no nilpotent, thus any Lagrangian sphere
L
with
[L]\neq 0\in QH(X)
is not a nilpotent. This implies
\beta_{L}\neq 0
, where
\beta_{L}
is the scalar in the cubic equation. Thus, for the
A_{m}
-configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{m}}
we have

\beta=\beta_{\mathscr{S}_{A_{m}}}=\beta_{S_{j}}\neq 0

by (
3.1.16
). Thus, by (
3.1.2
), each Lagrangian sphere
S_{j}
gives rise to two idempotents
e^{S_{j}}_{\pm}
, where

e^{S_{j}}_{\pm}=\pm\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\beta}}[S_{j}]+\frac{1}{8\beta}[S_{j}]^{2}.

(3.4.1)

From Lemma
3.1.4
, we have for every
j
,

\overline{\ell}_{S_{j}}(H)=\max\{\zeta_{e^{S_{j}}_{+}}(H),\ \zeta_{e^{S_{j}}_{-}}(H)\}

(3.4.2)

for any Hamiltonian
H
, and
S_{j}
is
e^{S_{j}}_{\pm}
-superheavy for any
j
. By Lemma
3.2.1
, the two idempotents corresponding to the Lagrangian spheres that are next to each other in the
A_{m}
-configuration, say
S_{j}
and
S_{j+1}
, share one of the two; without loss of generality we can assume

e^{S_{j}}_{+}=e^{S_{j+1}}_{-}.

(3.4.3)

To prove Theorem
C
, it is enough to prove the following.

Claim 3.4.1
.

The Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms

\{\zeta_{e^{S_{j}}_{+}}\}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant m-1}

are pairwise distinct.

We prove this claim. We need to prove that for any
i,j
such that
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant m-1
, we have
\zeta_{e^{S_{i}}_{+}}\neq\zeta_{e^{S_{j}}_{+}}
. For such
i,j
, we have that

•

S_{i}
is
e^{S_{i}}_{+}
-superheavy,

•

S_{j+1}
is
e^{S_{j}}_{+}
-superheavy, as
e^{S_{j}}_{+}=e^{S_{j+1}}_{-}
.

As
j-i\geqslant 1
, we have
S_{i}\cap S_{j+1}=\emptyset
. Thus,

\zeta_{e^{S_{i}}_{+}}\neq\zeta_{e^{S_{j}}_{+}}.

This proves Claim
3.4.1
and thus completes the proof of Theorem
C
.

∎

We now prove Theorem
1.3.2
. In
[
Kaw
, Theorem C(2)]
, the author proved the existence of three Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ham}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
. The proof uses a set of Lagrangian configurations arising from a toric degeneration studied by Y. Sun in
[
Sun20
]
that consists of a Lagrangian torus, an
A_{1}
-configuration, and an
A_{2}
-configuration. Here, we manage to improve
[
Kaw
, Theorem C(2)]
by completing the union of the
A_{1}
configuration and the
A_{2}
configuration to an
A_{4}
configuration by finding an additional Lagrangian sphere using some four (real) dimensional technique. This allows us to apply Theorem
C
and complete the proof.

Proof of Theorem
1.3.2
.

First, we find four linearly independent Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ham}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
, the universal cover of
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
. By considering the degeneration of the del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{D}_{4}
studied in
[
Sun20
, Appendix B, case of
X_{6}
]
, one obtains a monotone Lagrangian torus
L
, an
A_{2}
configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{2}}:=\{S_{1},S_{2}\}
, an
A_{1}
configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{1}}:=\{S_{3}\}
, that are mutually disjoint (i.e.
L\cap S_{1}=\emptyset
,
L\cap\left(S_{2}\cup S_{3}\right)=\emptyset
,
S_{1}\cap\left(S_{2}\cup S_{3}\right)=\emptyset
). By looking at the moment polytope of the degeneration, one can see that the homology classes represented by the
A_{2}
configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{2}}
and the
A_{1}
configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{1}}
are as follows
10
10

10

This was communicated to the author by Yuhan Sun.
:

\begin{gathered}=E_{2}-E_{3},\\
[S_{2}]=E_{3}-E_{4},\\
[S_{3}]=H-E_{2}-E_{3}-E_{4}.\end{gathered}

(3.4.4)

Now, consider the class
E_{4}-E_{1}
. By
[
BLW14
, Lemma 5.3]
, one can take a Lagrangian sphere
S
that represents the class
E_{4}-E_{1}
, i.e.

[S]=E_{4}-E_{1},

that is disjoint from the Lagrangian sphere
S_{1}
, i.e.
S_{1}\cap S=\emptyset
. By computing the intersection number, one can see that the Lagrangian spheres
S_{1},S_{2},S,S_{3}
form a
partial
A_{4}
configuration
in the sense that they satisfy the intersection property (
2.5.2
) expect for
S
, which instead satisfies the following weaker, homological version of the intersection property

[S]\cdot[S_{j}]=\begin{cases}1\text{ if $j=2,3$}\\
0\text{ if $j=1$}.\end{cases}

(3.4.5)

However, this is enough to obtain three Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms

\zeta_{e^{S_{1}}_{+}},\zeta_{e^{S_{2}}_{+}},\zeta_{e^{S}_{+}}=\zeta_{e^{S_{3}}_{-}}

which come from the Lagrangian spheres involved in the partial
A_{4}
configuration, and they are linearly independent, as the Lagrangian spheres
S_{1},S_{2},S,S_{3}
above satisfy
S_{1}\cap S=\emptyset,\ S_{2}\cap S_{3}=\emptyset
. This is enough to conclude
\zeta_{e^{S_{1}}_{+}}\neq\zeta_{e^{S_{2}}_{+}},\ \zeta_{e^{S_{2}}_{+}}\neq\zeta_{e^{S}_{+}}
. As it was shown in
[
Kaw
, Proof of Theorem C]
, there is an Entov–Polterovich quasimorphism
\zeta_{e^{L}}
for which the monotone Lagrangian torus
L
is
e^{L}
-superheavy. As the Lagrangian torus
L
, the
A_{2}
configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{2}}
, and the
A_{1}
configuration
\mathscr{S}_{A_{1}}
are all mutually disjoint, we conclude that

\{\zeta_{j}\}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant 4}:=\{\zeta_{e^{L}},\zeta_{e^{S_{1}}_{+}},\zeta_{e^{S_{2}}_{+}},\zeta_{e^{S}_{+}}=\zeta_{e^{S_{3}}_{-}}\}

(3.4.6)

are four linearly independent Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ham}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
. Evans proved that
\mathrm{Symp}_{0}(\mathbb{D}_{4})=\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
is weakly contractible, i.e.
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4}))=\{\mathrm{id}\}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}

[
Eva11
, Theorem 1.3]
. Thus, any quasimorphism on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ham}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
descends to a quasimorphism on
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
and thus,
\{\zeta_{j}\}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant 4}
defines four pairwise distinct Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms on
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
. As each of the four Entov–Polterovich quasimorphisms is Hofer Lipschitz continuous, we conclude that
\mathbb{R}^{4}
embeds quasi-isometrically to
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
, which in particular answers the Kapovich–Polterovich question for
X=\mathbb{D}_{4}
in the negative. The three quasimorphisms that are
C^{0}
and Hofer-Lipschitz continuous can be obtained as in
[
Kaw22
, Theorem 22]
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.3.2
.
∎

Remark 3.4.2.

It seems very likely that one can furthermore prove that quasimorphisms
\zeta_{e^{S_{1}}_{-}},\ \zeta_{e^{S_{3}}_{+}}
are pairwise distinct to the quasimorphisms in (
3.4.6
) and obtain six pairwise distinct quasimorphisms on
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
. We will just briefly outline the argument: given an
A_{m}
-configuration
\{L_{j}\}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant m}
in a del Pezzo surface, one can construct a new Lagrangian sphere
L_{m+1}
that completes the
A_{m}
configuration into a
m+1
-gon by taking a Lagrangian sphere representing the class
[L_{m+1}]=-(\sum_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}])
(Seidel considers this configuration of Lagrangian spheres in
[
Sei08
, Example 1.10]
). It should be able to show that
L_{m+1}
can be taken so that it is disjoint to
\{L_{j}\}_{2\leqslant j\leqslant m-1}
by a similar argument to above, which shows that
\zeta_{e^{S_{1}}_{-}},\ \zeta_{e^{S_{3}}_{+}}
are pairwise distinct to the quasimorphisms in (
3.4.6
) by superheaviness.

3.5
Proof of Theorem
D

In this section, we prove Theorem
D
. For the definition and the basic properties of the Dehn twist, we refer to
[
Sei97
]
.

Proof of Theorem
D
.

Case of even
n
:

First, notice that as
\beta
is a symplectic invariant and
\tau_{L}\in\mathrm{Symp}(X)
, we have

\beta_{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}=\beta_{L}.

Note that we have
\beta_{L}\neq 0
, as
QH(X,\omega)
is semi-simple. We denote them all by
\beta
, i.e.

\beta:=\beta_{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}=\beta_{L}\neq 0.

From (
3.1.2
), the idempotents induced by
\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})
are as follows.

\displaystyle e^{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\beta}}[\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})]+\frac{1}{8\beta}[\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})]^{2}

(3.5.1)

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\beta}}(\tau_{L})_{\ast}[L^{\prime}]+\frac{1}{8\beta}\left((\tau_{L})_{\ast}[L^{\prime}]\right)^{2}.

The Picard–Lefschetz formula implies

(\tau_{L})_{\ast}[L^{\prime}]=[L^{\prime}]-(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}([L]\cdot[L^{\prime}])[L],

(3.5.2)

and we also have

\begin{gathered}=2\sqrt{\beta}(e^{L}_{+}-e^{L}_{-}),\\
[L^{\prime}]=2\sqrt{\beta}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}).\end{gathered}

(3.5.3)

As Lagrangian spheres
\{L,L^{\prime}\}
form an
A_{2}
configuration, i.e.
|L\cap L^{\prime}|=1
, there are two possibilities for the intersection number, i.e.
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=\pm 1
. We have the following relation between the homological intersection number
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]
and the idempotents corresponding to
L
and
L^{\prime}
.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

Let
(X,\omega)
is a
2n
-dimensional monotone symplectic manifold with even
n
. Suppose there is an
A_{2}
configuration
\{L,L^{\prime}\}
. The idempotents corresponding to
L
and
L^{\prime}
, namely
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}
and
\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
, and the intersection number
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]
have the following relationship:

1.

(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=-1
if and only if
e^{L}_{+}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}
or
e^{L}_{-}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}
.

2.

(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=1
if and only if
e^{L}_{+}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}
or
e^{L}_{-}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}
.

Remark 3.5.2.
As pointed out in Remark
3.2
, only one of
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}
and
\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
is shared. See the proof of Proposition
3.5.1
for this.

We prove Proposition
3.5.1
at the end of this section, after the proof of Theorem
D
. First, we assume
(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=-1
. From Proposition
3.5.1
, this implies that we have either
e^{L}_{+}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}
or
e^{L}_{-}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}
. We assume the former, as the proof for the latter goes identically.

Putting equations (
3.5.2
) and (
3.5.3
) together, we get

\displaystyle(\tau_{L})_{\ast}[L^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=[L^{\prime}]+[L]

(3.5.4)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\beta}\left((e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L^{\prime}}_{-})+(e^{L}_{+}-e^{L}_{-})\right)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\beta}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L}_{-}).

From (
3.5.4
), we get

\displaystyle e^{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\beta}}[\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})]+\frac{1}{8\beta}[\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})]^{2}

(3.5.5)

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\beta}}2\sqrt{\beta}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L}_{-})+\frac{1}{8\beta}\left(2\sqrt{\beta}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L}_{-})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{2}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L}_{-})+\frac{1}{2}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}+e^{L}_{-})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\text{$e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}$\quad if $\pm=+$},\\
\text{$e^{L}_{-}$\quad if $\pm=-$}.\end{cases}

The equation (
3.5.5
) and Lemma
3.1.4
imply

\overline{\ell}_{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}=\max\{\zeta_{e^{L}_{-}},\zeta_{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}}\}.

(3.5.6)

Comparing it with

\begin{gathered}\overline{\ell}_{L}=\max\{\zeta_{e^{L}_{+}},\zeta_{e^{L}_{-}}\},\\
\overline{\ell}_{L^{\prime}}=\max\{\zeta_{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}},\zeta_{e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}}\},\end{gathered}

(3.5.7)

we obtain

\overline{\ell}_{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}\leqslant\max\{\overline{\ell}_{L},\overline{\ell}_{L^{\prime}}\}.

(3.5.8)

The other case where we have
(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=+1
can be dealt exactly in the same way.

Case of odd
n
:

As we have seen in the proof of Theorem
B
for the case of odd
n
, for any Lagrangian sphere
L
, we can find a distinguished unique unit
e^{L}
, which satisfies

\overline{\ell}_{L}=\zeta_{e^{L}}.

We have also seen that for any Lagrangian spheres satisfying
HF(L,L^{\prime})\neq 0
, we have
e^{L}=e^{L^{\prime}}
. Now, for an
A_{2}
-configuration
L,L^{\prime}
, we have
HF(L,L^{\prime})\neq 0
and
HF(L,\tau_{L}(L^{\prime}))\neq 0
, so we have

e^{L}=e^{L^{\prime}}=e^{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}.

Thus,

\overline{\ell}_{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}=\overline{\ell}_{L}=\overline{\ell}_{L^{\prime}}(=\zeta_{e^{L}}=\zeta_{e^{L^{\prime}}}=\zeta_{e^{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}}),

so we have

\overline{\ell}_{\tau_{L}(L^{\prime})}=\max\{\overline{\ell}_{L},\overline{\ell}_{L^{\prime}}\}.

(3.5.9)

We have completed the proof of Theorem
D
.
∎

We end this section with a proof of Proposition
3.5.1
which was used in the proof of Theorem
D
.

Proof of Proposition
3.5.1
.

First of all, the assumption
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]\neq 0
implies
\beta_{L}=\beta_{L^{\prime}}\neq 0
. We define

\beta:=\beta_{L}=\beta_{L^{\prime}}\neq 0.

By using

\begin{gathered}=2\beta^{1/2}(e^{L}_{+}-e^{L}_{-}),\\
[L^{\prime}]=2\beta^{1/2}(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}),\end{gathered}

(3.5.10)

we have

\displaystyle\cdot[L^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\int_{X}4\beta(e^{L}_{+}-e^{L}_{-})\cdot(e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L^{\prime}}_{-})

(3.5.11)

\displaystyle=4\beta\int_{X}(e^{L}_{+}\cdot e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L}_{-}\cdot e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}-e^{L}_{+}\cdot e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}+e^{L}_{-}\cdot e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}).

We will show that only one of the four terms survive. This is because only one of the two idempotents between
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}
and
\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
is shared, i.e.
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}\cap\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}\neq\emptyset
and
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}\neq\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
. To see this, first from Lemma
3.2.1
, we know that
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}\cap\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}\neq\emptyset
. However, we also have
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}\neq\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
, as if we had
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}=\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
, then (
3.5.10
) implies

[L]=\pm[L^{\prime}].

On one hand,
L,L^{\prime}
forming an
A_{2}
configuration implies

[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=\pm 1,

but on the other hand,
L
being an even-dimensional Lagrangian sphere implies

[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=[L]\cdot(\pm[L])=\pm 2,

which is a contradiction. Thus, we have proven that only one of the two idempotents between
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}
and
\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
is shared. From the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
, when two idempotents from
\{e^{L}_{+},\ e^{L}_{-}\}
and
\{e^{L^{\prime}}_{+},\ e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}\}
do not coincide, then they are orthogonal to each other, so only one of the four terms in (
3.5.11
) remains.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{X}e^{L}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(\pm\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\beta}}[L]+\frac{1}{8\beta}[L]^{2}\right)

(3.5.12)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\frac{1}{8\beta}[L]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\beta}\int_{X}[L]\cup[L]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\beta}[L]\cdot[L]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\beta}\cdot(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\chi(L)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\cdot\frac{1}{4\beta}.

Note that the second and the third equality follow from degree reasons, and the fifth equality uses the fact that
L
is a Lagrangian sphere,
\chi(L)
is the Euler characteristic of
L
, i.e. two. The same property applies for
L^{\prime}
:

\int_{X}e^{L^{\prime}}_{\pm}=(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\cdot\frac{1}{4\beta}.

(3.5.13)

Now, it follows from (
3.5.11
), (
3.5.12
), and (
3.5.13
) that the intersection number
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]
satisfies the following:

•

e^{L}_{+}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}
or
e^{L}_{-}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}
if and only if
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=-(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}.

•

e^{L}_{+}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{+}
or
e^{L}_{-}=e^{L^{\prime}}_{-}
if and only if
[L]\cdot[L^{\prime}]=(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}.

This completes the proof of Proposition
3.5.1
.

∎

Remark 3.5.3.
A recent work of Biran–Cornea
[
BC21
, Lemma 5.3.1]
contains a result that is based on a spirit similar to Theorem
D
. Although they work in a different setting, namely with exact Lagrangians in a Liouville manifold, it would be interesting to study if one can obtain a similar inequality to Theorem
D
by their method.
